# Gate Specifications & Enforcement Rules

This document establishes the normative enforcement rules, detection capabilities, limits, and configuration keys for all gates in `discipline`.

**Superseded when:** A gate rule is amended, a new gate ships, or language-pack detection boundaries expand. Update in place; do not fork.

---

## Gate Catalog

<!-- generated:gates -->
| Gate id | Suite | Status | Languages | Rule Description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`agents-md`](#agents-md) | agent-guard | **shipped** | any | AGENTS.md exists; CLAUDE.md / GEMINI.md do not fork it |
| [`assertion-reduction`](#assertion-reduction) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, PHPT, Java, Go, PHP, C/C++, C#, Ruby, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C | assertion count / strength must not drop in an existing test |
| [`vacuous-tests`](#vacuous-tests) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, PHPT, Java, Go, PHP, C/C++, C#, Ruby, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C | new tests must carry a non-tautological assertion |
| [`ignored-tests`](#ignored-tests) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, PHPT, Java, Go, PHP, C/C++, C#, Ruby, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C | tests must not be newly #[ignore]d or skipped without directive |
| [`unsafe-safety-comment`](#unsafe-safety-comment) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust | unsafe blocks / impls carry a // SAFETY: comment |
| [`deletion-rationale`](#deletion-rationale) | agent-guard | **shipped** | any | deleted files and removed tests need a scoped removes: rationale |
| [`time-estimates`](#time-estimates) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | no calendar / duration estimates in markdown or the PR body |
| [`pii`](#pii) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | no home paths, LAN IPs, denylisted hostnames, or fixed-format credentials (private keys, AWS, GitHub, Slack, OpenAI and Anthropic tokens, literal bearer headers) in tracked text |
| [`agent-scratch`](#agent-scratch) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | agent scratch state is never tracked |
| [`shell-secrets`](#shell-secrets) | hygiene | **shipped** | shell, docker, workflows | no command-line secrets or unverified piped scripts in shell, docker, or CI |
| [`issue-link`](#issue-link) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | PR title or description links a tracking issue (#123, Fixes #123) |
| [`review-threads`](#review-threads) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | the pull request has no unresolved review thread |
| [`ratified-paths`](#ratified-paths) | agent-guard | **shipped** | any | edits to protected paths carry an owner's ratification on an issue the pull request closes |
| [`citation-metadata`](#citation-metadata) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | CITATION.cff and .zenodo.json are valid, agree with each other, and cite the concept DOI |
| [`commit-provenance`](#commit-provenance) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | commits carry the required trailers; an agent-produced commit carries a review by someone else |
| [`config-integrity`](#config-integrity) | integrity | **shipped** | any | a change cannot weaken its own discipline.toml without a token |
| [`stub-bodies`](#stub-bodies) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, Go, Java, C#, PHP, Ruby, C/C++, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C | added functions are not stubs; existing bodies are not replaced by todo!() / NotImplementedError / return null |
| [`harness-tampering`](#harness-tampering) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Go, Python, JS/TS, Rust | a file the test runner loads does not make a failing run pass |
| [`error-swallowing`](#error-swallowing) | agent-guard | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, Go, Java, C#, PHP, Ruby, C/C++, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C | no new empty error handler or discarded Result outside tests |
| [`instruction-smuggling`](#instruction-smuggling) | agent-guard | **shipped** | any (invisible characters, instruction files); Rust, Python, JS/TS, Go, Java, C#, PHP, Ruby, C/C++, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C and prose files (phrases) | no invisible Unicode, unreviewed agent-instruction edits, or instruction-like text in comments and prose |
| [`build-hooks`](#build-hooks) | integrity | **shipped** | package.json, build.rs, setup.py, .npmrc, .pypirc, pip.conf, .cargo/config.toml, .env* | install and build hooks that gain network or shell access, and package-manager configuration edits, need a token |
| [`toolchain-config`](#toolchain-config) | integrity | **shipped** | tsconfig, ruff, mypy, pytest, coverage, flake8, Cargo lints, rustflags, nextest, eslintrc, golangci, jest, codecov, phpstan, phpunit, and compiler warning flags in Makefile, CMake, setup.py and build.rs | compiler, linter, type-checker, test-runner and coverage configuration cannot be loosened without a token |
| [`sandbox-config`](#sandbox-config) | integrity | **shipped** | Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Qwen Code, OpenCode, Cursor and Copilot CLI settings, MCP server lists, devcontainer.json, Docker Compose, CI job and service containers | a change cannot widen an agent's permissions or sandbox, or a container's isolation, without a token |
| [`scope-confinement`](#scope-confinement) | agent-guard | **shipped** | any | changes stay inside authorized paths |
| [`suppression-delta`](#suppression-delta) | agent-guard | **shipped** | per pack | newly added linter / compiler suppression annotations |
| [`provenance-tags`](#provenance-tags) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | published numerics carry (measured|target|projected) |
| [`ci-integrity`](#ci-integrity) | integrity | **shipped** | any | workflow weakening: continue-on-error, || true, unpinned actions |
| [`ci-skip-set`](#ci-skip-set) | integrity | **shipped** | any | rollup skip set matches each job's `if:` under the observed filter outputs |
| [`test-floor`](#test-floor) | integrity | **shipped** | any | test-count ratchet read from the base ref |
| [`golden-output`](#golden-output) | integrity | **shipped** | any | prevents stealth edits to committed golden/test output files without explicit override |
| [`dependency-delta`](#dependency-delta) | integrity | **shipped** | any | manifest diff inspection: zero wildcards, source/license allowlists, and deny.toml verification |
| [`test-budget`](#test-budget) | integrity | **shipped** | Rust, Python, JS/TS, Go, any | property-test and fuzz effort ratchet (cases, shrink iters, fuzztime, seed corpus) |
| [`pr-checklist`](#pr-checklist) | hygiene | **shipped** | any | ticked PR checkboxes are reconciled against the diff |
| [`command`](#command) | verification | **shipped** | any | fail-closed wrapper for any tool: zero-tests guard, canary, count ratchet |
| [`sanitizers`](#sanitizers) | verification | **shipped** | Rust, C/C++ | ASan / TSan preset with audited suppressions and a race canary |
| [`msrv`](#msrv) | quality | **shipped** | Rust | cargo check under the pinned MSRV |
| [`miri`](#miri) | verification | **shipped** | Rust | Miri tiers with zero-tests guard |
| [`unsafe-budget`](#unsafe-budget) | verification | **shipped** | Rust | unsafe count ratchet |
| [`bench-regression`](#bench-regression) | bench | **shipped** | Rust, Go, Python, C/C++ | benchmark drift via harness adapters (deterministic counts or BCa intervals) |
| [`archive-contents`](#archive-contents) | integrity | **shipped** | any | distribution archive must contain required paths and zero forbidden developer artifacts |
| [`manifest-sync`](#manifest-sync) | integrity | **shipped** | any | reconcile git-tracked files against packaging manifest declarations |
| [`version-lockstep`](#version-lockstep) | integrity | **shipped** | any | version declarations across headers, manifests, and files must remain in lockstep |

### Finding Codes

Every finding carries a code, `gate/code` (`ci-integrity/unpinned-action`, `vacuous-tests/vacuous-test-added`): the `code` field of the JSON report and the `[gate/code]` heading of the `agent-prompt` report that hooks and `discipline mcp` hand to agents. `discipline explain <gate>` lists a gate's codes. The code names what the finding protects, not how it is detected: a gate that learns to catch more cases of the same problem keeps the code. Codes are registered in `src/findings.rs`; the title beside a code is display text. A rule id that starts with `policy/` in a SARIF, JUnit or GitLab report is not a finding code: it is a policy refusal (an override budget, a required approval, a hidden directive that was not read), which no gate reports and no directive lifts (`docs/CONFIGURATION.md`, "Policy Refusals").

**Titles.** A title is display text and may be reworded in any release; nothing keys on it. Titles follow one grammar, which `src/findings.rs` tests: Title Case with every word capitalized, ASCII only, no data (paths, counts, versions and names go in the message), unique within a gate. A title names either something present at head (`Hardcoded Secret (GitHub Token)`) or a change (`Verification Job Removed`); a trailing parenthesis holds one value from a closed set: the mechanism in its literal spelling (`(continue-on-error)`, `(--locked)`) or a class (`(tests)`). Verbs keep one meaning each: *Added* a new entry; *Removed* an entry gone, *Deleted* a file gone; *Missing* required but absent; *Changed* any edit; *Weakened* a construct kept but made weaker; *Loosened* a threshold relaxed; *Widened* scope or authority grown; *Narrowed* verification coverage shrunk; *Increased* / *Decreased* a count or size; *Masked* a failure hidden; *Regressed* a measurement worsened; *Detected* found at head.

### Directive Policy

Each gate has at most one canonical directive (with at most one documented deprecated spelling); `allow-test-shrink` serves both `test-floor` and `test-budget`. A few gates (`unsafe-safety-comment`, `agents-md`, `pii`, `agent-scratch`, `ci-skip-set`, `time-estimates`) have no directive: they are lifted by fixing the finding, an inline marker where the gate documents one, or `exempt_paths`. Directives must be scoped to their natural subject (file path, test name, action ref, workflow job, dependency name, or rule identifier). Blanket waivers without subjects are rejected. The subject is the first thing after the colon and the rest is the reason: `allow-ignore: test_sync flaky on the shared runner` lifts a finding on `test_sync`, and `allow-ignore: test_other skipped the way test_sync was` does not, because a word of the reason never names a subject. One directive line names one subject: there is no list form, so two subjects take two lines ([Syntax & Grammar](CONFIGURATION.md#syntax--grammar)).

---

## Language Scope & Detection Boundaries

Most gates are language-independent and inspect text, git diffs, configuration, workflows, or repository metadata; the *Languages* column of the catalog above is the authority for each. The AST gates (`assertion-reduction`, `vacuous-tests`, `ignored-tests`, `unsafe-safety-comment`, `error-swallowing`, `stub-bodies`, `harness-tampering`, `suppression-delta`, and the phrase tier of `instruction-smuggling`) operate through tree-sitter AST extraction.

**What no gate here catches.** Every gate reads a change. Code that is built to the tests its author can see, without editing them (overfitting to the visible suite), passes all of them: nothing in the diff is weaker than before. The control is held-out tests the author of the change cannot see, which the repository owns (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §1.4; see SpecBench, arXiv:2605.21384).

When a change touches source files in a language without an active pack, each AST gate **names the unanalysed files in its report notes** (F7) rather than rendering a silent zero.

### Language Packs

| Language | Test function patterns | Assertion vocabulary (strong = equality / pattern) | Skip markers (`ignored-tests`) | Suppression markers (`suppression-delta`) | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Rust** | `#[test]`, `#[tokio::test]`, `#[async_std::test]`, `#[rstest]` | `assert*!`, `debug_assert*!`, `prop_assert*!`; strong: `_eq`, `_ne`, `matches` | `#[ignore]`, `#[cfg_attr(..., ignore)]`; conditional: `#[cfg_attr(<predicate>, ignore)]`, a `#[cfg(..)]` on a Cargo feature | `#[allow(...)]`, `#[expect(...)]` (the `// SAFETY:` rule is `unsafe-safety-comment`) | **shipped** |
| **Python** | pytest / unittest collection rules: `test*` functions, `test*` methods of `Test*` classes and `TestCase` subclasses (`self_test()` is not a test unless `[tests] functions` declares it) | `assert` statements, `self.assert*`, `pytest.raises`, `pytest.approx`; strong: `==`, `assertEqual` family | `@pytest.mark.skip` / `xfail`, `@unittest.skip`; conditional: `@pytest.mark.skipif`, `@pytest.mark.xfail(<condition>)`, `@unittest.skipIf` / `skipUnless`, a name the file binds to one of these marks, `pytest.skip()` under an `if` | `# type: ignore`, `# noqa`, `# pragma: no cover` | **shipped** |
| **JavaScript / TypeScript** | `test(` / `it(` callbacks (Jest, Vitest, Mocha, node:test) | `expect(...).matcher`, `assert.*`; strong: `toBe`, `toEqual`, `toStrictEqual`; weak: `toBeTruthy`, `toBeDefined` | `.skip`, `.todo`, `xit`, `xdescribe`, Mocha `this.skip()`; conditional: `.skipIf(..)`, `.runIf(..)` | `@ts-ignore`, `@ts-expect-error`, `@ts-nocheck`, `eslint-disable*`, `istanbul ignore` / `c8 ignore` | **shipped** |
| **Golden (PHPT)** | Standard PHPT sections (`--TEST--`, `--FILE--`, `--EXPECT--`) | Exact expectation sections (`--EXPECT--`, `--EXPECTF--`, `--EXPECTREGEX--`) | `--SKIPIF--`, `--XFAIL--` | — | **shipped** |
| **Java** | `@Test`, `@ParameterizedTest`, `@RepeatedTest` (JUnit 4/5, TestNG) | JUnit `assert*`, AssertJ `assertThat(...)`; strong: `assertEquals`, `assertThrows`, `isEqualTo` | `@Disabled`, `@Ignore`, `@Test(enabled = false)`; conditional: JUnit 5 `@DisabledIf*` / `@EnabledIf*` / `@DisabledOn*` / `@EnabledOn*`, `assumeTrue` / `assumeFalse` / `assumingThat` / `assumeThat` / `assumeNotNull`, also under an `if` | `@SuppressWarnings` | **shipped** |
| **Kotlin** | JUnit 4 / 5 and TestNG `@Test`, `@ParameterizedTest`, `@RepeatedTest`, `@TestFactory`; Kotest `StringSpec` / `FunSpec` / `DescribeSpec` / `ShouldSpec` / `ExpectSpec` / `FeatureSpec` / `BehaviorSpec` bodies; `test*` functions in a test path | kotlin.test and JUnit `assert*`, `assert(...)`, AssertJ / Truth `assertThat`, Kotest `shouldBe` and the other `should*` matchers (infix or call), `assertThrows<E> { }` / `shouldThrow<E> { }`; strong: `assertEquals`, `shouldBe`, `assertThat`; tautology: `assertTrue(true)`, `assertEquals(x, x)`, `x shouldBe x` | `@Disabled`, `@Ignore`, `@Test(enabled = false)`, class-level `@Disabled`, Kotest `"!name"`, `xtest` / `xit` / `xdescribe`, `.config(enabled = false)`; conditional: the JUnit 5 annotations and assumptions of the Java row | `@Suppress`, `@SuppressWarnings`, `@SuppressLint` | **shipped** |
| **Swift** | XCTest `func test*()` in an `XCTestCase` subclass (any type in a test path); Swift Testing `@Test` | `XCTAssert*`, `XCTFail`, `XCTUnwrap`, `#expect`, `#require`; strong: `XCTAssertEqual`, `#expect(a == b)`, `#require` | `throw XCTSkip(..)`, the `.disabled(..)` trait; conditional: `XCTSkip` under `if` / `guard`, `XCTSkipIf(..)`, `XCTSkipUnless(..)`, `.disabled(if: ..)`, `.enabled(if: ..)` | `// swiftlint:disable` | **shipped** |
| **Scala** | ScalaTest `test("x") { }`, `"x" should "y" in { }`, WordSpec / FunSpec nesting; MUnit `test("x") { }`; specs2 `"x" >> { }`; JUnit `@Test` | `assert`, `assertEquals`, `assertResult`, `intercept[E]`, `assertThrows[E]`, `fail`, `should` / `must` matchers; strong: `assert(a == b)`, `assertEquals`, `shouldBe x` | `ignore("x")`, `... ignore { }`, `test("x".ignore)`, `pending`, `cancel(...)`; conditional: `cancel(..)` / `pending` under an `if`, `assume(..)` | `@nowarn`, `@SuppressWarnings`, `// scalastyle:off`, `// scalafix:off` / `ok` | **shipped** |
| **Objective-C** | XCTest `- (void)test*` with no parameters in an `XCTestCase` subclass (a `*Tests` class, or any class in a test path) | `XCTAssert*`, `XCTFail`; strong: `XCTAssertEqual`, `XCTAssertEqualObjects` | `XCTSkip(..)`; conditional: `XCTSkip` under an `if`, `XCTSkipIf(..)`, `XCTSkipUnless(..)` | `#pragma clang diagnostic ignored`, `// NOLINT` | **shipped** |
| **C / C++** | GoogleTest `TEST*`, Catch2 `TEST_CASE`, doctest, C ABI smoke (`main`, `test_*`) | `EXPECT_*` / `ASSERT_*`, `REQUIRE` / `CHECK`, `assert(...)`; strong: `_EQ`, `_STREQ`, comparison operators | `DISABLED_` prefix, `GTEST_SKIP()`, Catch2 `SKIP()` / `[.]`, Unity `TEST_IGNORE()` / `TEST_IGNORE_MESSAGE(..)`; conditional: the skip calls under an `if` | `// NOLINT` | **shipped** |
| **Go** | `func Test*(t *testing.T)`, subtests | `t.Error*` / `t.Fatal*`, testify `assert.*` / `require.*`; strong: `Equal`, `DeepEqual` | `t.Skip*`; conditional: `t.Skip*` under an `if` | `//nolint`, `//lint:ignore`, `revive:disable` | **shipped** |
| **PHP** | PHPUnit `test*` methods, `@test` docblock / attribute (a top-level `test*` function only under a test path or a `[tests] paths` glob), Pest `test(` / `it(` | PHPUnit `assert*`, Pest matchers (`->toBe`, `->toEqual`); strong: `assertEquals`, `assertSame`, `assertCount` | `$this->markTestSkipped()`, `$this->markTestIncomplete()`, `->skip()`; conditional: `markTestSkipped()` / `markTestIncomplete()` under an `if`, `#[Requires*]` attributes | `// @psalm-suppress`, `// @phpstan-ignore`, `// phpcs:ignore` | **shipped** |
| **C#** | `[Fact]`, `[Theory]` (xUnit), `[Test]` (NUnit), `[TestMethod]` (MSTest) | `Assert.*`, `StringAssert.*`, `CollectionAssert.*`; strong: `Equal`, `Same`, `AreEqual` / `AreSame` (NUnit, MSTest), `Assert.That(a, Is.EqualTo(b))` / `Is.SameAs`, `Throws` | `[Ignore]`, `[Fact(Skip = "...")]`, `Assert.Ignore(..)`, `Assert.Skip(..)`; conditional: those calls under an `if`, `Assume.That(..)`, `Skip.If(..)` / `Skip.IfNot(..)`, `Assert.SkipWhen(..)` / `Assert.SkipUnless(..)`, `[Fact(Skip = "..", SkipWhen = nameof(X))]` / `SkipUnless` | `#pragma warning disable`, `[SuppressMessage]` | **shipped** |
| **Ruby** | `def test_*` (Minitest, Test::Unit), `it` / `specify` (RSpec) | `assert_*`, `refute_*`, RSpec `expect(...).to eq(...)`; strong: `assert_equal`, `eq` | `xit`, `xdescribe`, `:skip`, `skip`, `omit`, `pending`; conditional: those calls under `if` / `unless` (statement or modifier), a ternary or `&&` / `||`, `omit_if(..)`, `omit_unless(..)` | `# rubocop:disable` | **shipped** |

## Confidence & Stakes Severity Hierarchy

Discipline organizes violation severity into a 3-tier hierarchy based on detection confidence and operational stakes:

1. **`error` (Blocking, Exit 1):** High-confidence violations representing clear security compromises, assertion drops, or intentional test erosions. Fails CI by default.
2. **`warning` (Non-blocking, Exit 0 unless `--fail-on-warnings`):** Heuristic or prose scans where context matters, or performance/benchmark gates that may fluctuate across hardware. Visible in all reports; promoted to blocking with `--fail-on-warnings`.
3. **`note` (Informational, Exit 0):** Advisory annotations (e.g. conditional skips on target platforms, stale baseline entries, unanalysed languages). Mapped to `note` in SARIF, `info` in GitLab Code Quality, and GitHub Actions `notice` annotations.

### Default Severity by Gate

Default enablement and default severity are part of the compatibility contract (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §3.1): within a major version a default may only become stricter unless the change is recorded in the [Default Changes ledger](ROADMAP.md#default-changes-compatibility-ledger). `tests/test_config.rs::default_enablement_and_severity_match_snapshot` pins every row below, so a default cannot change without a deliberate edit that shows up in review.

A default is chosen from two inputs: **detection confidence** (how often a finding on an arbitrary repository is a real defect) and the **cost of being wrong** (a false block stops an unrelated merge; a missed finding lets erosion through). A gate whose finding population in an unknown repository is high because the flagged construct is also a routine, reviewed practice defaults to `warning`: it stays visible in every report and blocks under `--fail-on-warnings` or an explicit `severity = "error"`.

**Default-on gates:**

| Gate | Default | Detection confidence | Cost of being wrong | Rationale |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `assertion-reduction` | on, `error` | High: AST count and strength comparison of the same test on base and head. | False block: a legitimate test refactor needs a scoped `allow-assertion-drop:` directive. Miss: silent loss of coverage. | The construct flagged (a test losing assertions) is rare in reviewed changes and is the core erosion this tool exists to stop. `self-comparison-assertion-introduced` is a `warning` whatever the gate's severity: it matches an exact form, and a deliberate reflexivity or not-NaN check has that form. |
| `vacuous-tests` | on, `error` | High: AST detection of a new test with no non-tautological assertion. | False block: an assertion helper not yet listed in `assert_helper_fns`. Miss: a test that can never fail. | Only new tests are judged, so the population is bounded by the change itself. `self-comparison-assertion-added` is a `warning` whatever the gate's severity, for the reason given for `assertion-reduction`; a test of nothing but self-comparisons is the vacuous test, at the gate's severity. |
| `ignored-tests` | on, `error` (conditional skips: `note`; CI-conditional skips: `error`, set by `ci_skip_severity`) | High: AST skip markers on tests added or changed in the diff. | False block: an intentional skip needs `allow-ignore:`. Miss: a disabled test counted as passing. | Platform-predicated skips are already downgraded to `note`; unconditional skips are rare and deliberate. |
| `unsafe-safety-comment` | on, `error` | High: AST `unsafe` block or impl without a preceding `// SAFETY:` comment. | False block: a missing comment on a sound block, fixed by writing it. Miss: an undocumented soundness invariant. | Only diff-touched unsafe sites are checked; writing the comment is the fix. |
| `deletion-rationale` | on, `error` | High: git records the deletion exactly. | False block: a planned removal needs one `removes:` line. Miss: a stealth deletion of tests or benchmarks. | Deletions are infrequent and the fix is a single scoped line. |
| `pii` | on, `error` | High for home paths, LAN IPs, denylisted hosts and fixed-format tokens; `diff_only = false`, so the whole tracked tree is swept. | False block: a pre-existing documentation path. Miss: a leaked workstation path or internal host in a public repository. | A leak is not reversible once published. Brownfield adopters use `diff_only = true`, `allowed_users`, or the grandfathering baseline. |
| `agent-scratch` | on, `error` | High: a tracked path matching agent state directories. | False block: a deliberately committed directory of the same name, exempted by path. Miss: private agent state in history. | The path set is narrow and the committed state is not reversible once pushed. |
| `shell-secrets` | on, `error` (token rules) / `warning` (heuristic rules) | High for structured tokens; heuristic for argv and pipe patterns. | False block: a token-shaped test fixture, exempted by path. Miss: a live credential in history. | The split already downgrades the heuristic rules at finding level. |
| `config-integrity` | on, `error` | High: base and head configuration are diffed structurally. | False block: an intended loosening needs `allow-gate-weakening:`. Miss: a change lowering its own bar (F9). | The gate protects every other gate; it cannot be advisory. |
| `instruction-smuggling` | on, `error` (invisible characters, instruction files) / `warning` (phrases, encoded blobs) | High for a bidi override or a zero-width character: the code point is either there or not. High for an instruction-file edit: the path is the fact. Low for a phrase: a paraphrase defeats it. | False block: a localisation table with directional marks needs `exempt_paths` or a directive; every `AGENTS.md` edit needs a directive. Miss: any injection that avoids the phrase list. | What a reviewer sees must be what the parser reads; what the next agent reads must be reviewed as code. The phrase tier is a tripwire and is reported as one. |
| `error-swallowing` | on, `error` | High: the handler body is read from the syntax tree; base and head are compared per file as a multiset, so a moved handler is not new. | False block: a deliberate best-effort handler needs `allow-swallow:` or an inline marker. Miss: a discarded result the language does not mark (`_`); a Rust `let _ = <call>` whose fallible callee is off the known-fallible name list is reported at `warning`, not blocked. | An empty `catch` or a discarded `Result` is how a failure an agent cannot fix stops surfacing. |
| `stub-bodies` | on, `error` | High: the body is read from the syntax tree and is the whole body; base and head are compared per function. | False block: a deliberate placeholder needs `allow-stub:`. Miss: a stub that carries one extra statement, or a body that special-cases the inputs its tests use. | An added `todo!()` or a body replaced by `return null` is the change no other gate sees. |
| `build-hooks` | on, `error` | High for a lifecycle script or a manager-config path: the JSON key or the path is the fact. Medium for a build-script line: token match on added lines. | False block: a `prepare: husky` hook or a private-registry `.npmrc` needs `allow-build-hook:`. Miss: a hook that shells out through a script file the token list does not see. | Code that runs on every install is the workflow an agent can still reach after `ci-integrity` closes the workflow files. |
| `toolchain-config` | on, `error` | High for a data file: base and head are diffed structurally against a per-tool rule table. A configuration written as code is reported at `warning` as changed, not analysed. | False block: an intended loosening needs `allow-toolchain-weakening:`. Miss: a lint or type bar lowered in the same change that would have failed it. | Same stakes as `ci-integrity` dropping `-D warnings`, one file over. |
| `sandbox-config` | on, `error` | High for a data file: base and head are diffed structurally against a per-agent rule table, and a mode is judged against the value an absent key takes. A firewall script is reported at `warning` as changed, not analysed. | False block: an intended widening (a new allowed command, an MCP server) needs `allow-sandbox-widening:`. Miss: a key or a value the table does not model. | A widened sandbox removes the boundary every other control assumes. |
| `ci-integrity` | on, `error` | High for `continue-on-error`, `\|\| true` and unpinned actions in modified workflows (`diff_only = true`). | False block: an intended pattern needs `allow-ci-weakening:`. Miss: a rollup that reports green while a job is skipped. | Only modified workflows are scanned by default, so pre-existing patterns do not block adoption. |
| `ci-skip-set` | on, `error` | High: each `needs` result is compared to its job's `if:` evaluated over the observed filter outputs; an unmodelled term is a finding, not a guess. Inert (a named "not evaluated" note) unless the rollup job supplies `DISCIPLINE_CI_CONTEXT`. | False block: a rollup whose workflow uses an `if:` form outside the modelled subset. Miss: a skip set the evaluator cannot distinguish from a legitimate one (all filters false on a change that touches no filtered path), reported as a note. | Supplying the context is the opt-in, so enabling it by default costs an ordinary diff check nothing. |
| `test-floor` | on, `error` | High: the base-ref test count is the floor unless one is configured. | False block: a test consolidation needs `allow-test-shrink:`. Miss: silent test-count erosion. | The ratchet is relative to the base ref, so it never fails a repository for its existing state. |
| `golden-output` | on, `error` | High: an edit to a committed golden or snapshot file. | False block: a legitimate snapshot update needs `allow-golden-update:`. Miss: an expectation rewritten to match a regression. | Snapshot edits are exactly the change a reviewer must see called out. |
| `dependency-delta` | on, `error` | High: manifest diffs are parsed; only changed dependencies are judged. | False block: a new dependency outside the allow-list needs `allow-dependency:` or an allow-list entry. Miss: a wildcard or unpinned git dependency. | The judged population is the dependencies the change adds. |
| `test-budget` | on, `error` | High: property-test and fuzz budgets compared base to head. | False block: an intended budget cut needs `allow-test-shrink:`. Miss: a fuzz or proptest budget quietly reduced. | Budgets are rarely edited and a cut is a deliberate decision. |
| `command` | on, `error` | Inert until a `command`, `preset`, or `DISCIPLINE_COMMAND` is configured; then the configured tool's own verdict. | False block: only a failure of the tool the repository chose to run. Miss: none while inert. | Enabling it by default costs nothing; configuring it is the opt-in. |
| `suppression-delta` | on, **`warning`** | Syntactically high, semantically low: `#[allow(...)]` is the reviewed escape hatch from `clippy -D warnings`, and `# noqa` / `// nolint` are routine. | False block at `error`: 78 findings (measured) across one consumer's last 100 merged pull requests, nearly all reviewed and intended. Miss at `warning`: none; the finding is still reported. | The flagged construct is a normal reviewed practice, so blocking by default fails ordinary changes. Repositories that treat every new suppression as a defect set `severity = "error"` (this repository does). |
| `time-estimates` | on, `warning` | Heuristic prose match over the whole tree (`diff_only = false`). | False block at `error`: at v0.4.2, 51 findings (measured) in one consumer repository and 26 (measured) in another, nearly all pre-existing documentation. | Prose context decides whether a duration is an estimate. |
| `bench-regression` | on, `warning` | Depends on the adapter: deterministic counts are high, wall-clock intervals are hardware-sensitive. | False block: runner jitter on wall-clock benchmarks. Miss: a real regression, still reported. | Projects with deterministic counters set `severity = "error"`. |
| `citation-metadata` | on, `error` | High: both files are parsed and their shape is checked against the Citation File Format 1.2.0 and Zenodo's deposit vocabulary; ORCID check digits are computed. The concept-DOI rule relies on how `identifiers` describes each DOI. | False block: none known; a CFF 1.2.0 feature outside the checked subset is ignored, not rejected. Miss: a DOI that is well formed but does not resolve, or names another work. | A repository with neither file examines nothing, and only a change that edits one is judged, so enabling it costs nothing elsewhere. |
| `agents-md` | on, `warning` | High, but the finding is documentation hygiene, not a code defect. | False block: a repository without an agent guide fails every change. | Missing or forked guidance does not make a change unsafe. |

**Default-off gates** (`issue-link`, `review-threads`, `ratified-paths`, `commit-provenance`, `provenance-tags`, `pr-checklist`, `scope-confinement`, `archive-contents`, `manifest-sync`, `version-lockstep`, `sanitizers`, `miri`, `unsafe-budget`, `msrv`) need repository-specific input (a tracker convention, a forge token, protected paths and their owners, archive path, manifest rules, version sources, toolchain) or encode a policy most repositories do not hold. They default to `error` so that enabling one is a single `enabled = true` line that blocks. `harness-tampering` is off for another reason: it is new, and a gate that is on by default changes the verdict of the next run of every repository that adopts a release, so turning it on is a default change of its own, recorded in the [Default Changes ledger](ROADMAP.md#default-changes-compatibility-ledger) when it is decided (#481). Its three exact forms (a Go `TestMain` that drops the result, a pytest hook that rewrites outcomes or removes items, a replaced `unittest` result method) report at the gate's severity, `error`: each one makes failing tests report success, and severity follows that, not how often the form appears. Its fourth, an exit with status zero in a harness file, never reports above `warning`: the same call ends a healthy tear-down in working repositories, and no measured false-positive rate on a stated corpus supports blocking on it. Raising it is a change to a severity that consumers key on, and goes through a deprecation, not a silent change.

### Finding-Level Severity Overrides

Certain gates distinguish high-confidence rules from heuristic indicators within the same gate:

- **`shell-secrets`:**
  - **High-confidence token rules (gate severity, `error` by default):** Structured secrets (`SECRET-TOKEN-GITHUB` `ghp_` / `github_pat_`, `SECRET-TOKEN-AWS` `AKIA...` or a literal `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=`, `SECRET-TOKEN-SLACK` `xox[baprs]-`, `SECRET-TOKEN-OPENAI` OpenAI and Anthropic `sk-...` keys, `SECRET-KEY-BLOCK` PEM private-key headers).
  - **Heuristic rules (`warning`):** Command-line arguments, pipe constructs and literal assignments (`ARGV-ENV`, `ARGV-DOCKER`, `ARGV-INLINE`, `INJECT-PIPE`, `INJECT-XARGS`, `SECRET-ARGV-PASSWORD`, `SECRET-LITERAL-BEARER`, `SECRET-LITERAL-ENV`).
- **`ignored-tests`:**
  - **Unconditional skips (`error`):** Tests newly disabled via `#[ignore]`, `@pytest.mark.skip`, `xit`, or `@Disabled` without justification.
  - **Conditional target skips (`note`):** Platform-predicated skips (`#[cfg_attr(windows, ignore)]`, `skipif(sys.platform == 'win32')`, `@DisabledOnOs(OS.WINDOWS)`).
  - **CI-conditional skips (`ci_skip_severity`, the gate's severity unless set):** Skips under a condition that holds in CI (`skipif(os.environ.get("CI"))`, `test.skipIf(process.env.CI)`, `@DisabledIfEnvironmentVariable(named = "CI", ..)`).
- **`harness-tampering`:**
  - **Exact forms (gate severity, `error` by default):** `test-main-result-discarded`, `pytest-hook-masks-results`, `unittest-result-method-replaced`.
  - **Exit with status zero (`warning` at most):** `harness-exits-zero`, whatever the gate's severity is set to.
- **`pii` / Workstation Hygiene:**
  - Private RFC 1918 LAN IPs (`192.168.x.x`, `10.x.x.x`, `172.16.x.x`) are reported at the gate's severity (`error` by default) and echoed in the finding unless `redact_lan_ips = true`; `gates.pii.lan_ips = false` turns the rule off.

---

## Shipped Gates

### Pillar 1: Agent Conformance and Diff Guard (`agent-guard`)

#### `assertion-reduction`
- **Rule:** For each test present on both sides (matched by module-qualified name within a file, or by name across files for moved tests), neither the count of effective assertions nor the count of strong assertions may drop.
- **Languages:** Rust, Python, JavaScript / TypeScript, PHPT, Java, Go, PHP, C/C++, C#, Ruby, Kotlin, Swift, Scala, Objective-C.
- **What it catches:**
  - Deleting assertion statements or macros within existing tests.
  - Assertion weakening (e.g. `assert_eq!(a, b)` -> `assert!(a == b)` or `assert!(a.is_some())`).
  - Replacing strong matchers with truthiness checks (e.g. `expect(x).toEqual(y)` -> `expect(x).toBeTruthy()`).
  - Replacing assertions with tautologies (`assert!(true)`, `assert_eq!(x, x)`).
  - A file the grammar cannot fully read: C, C++, C# and Objective-C report `Source File Parsed With Errors (preprocessor)` (warning, with the error-region count in the notes); in other languages `Source File Parsed With Errors` blocks when the file could hide a test (a test path, tests on either side, or Rust, whose tests live in source files) and is a warning otherwise.
  - `Fatal Assertions Weakened To Non-Fatal` (warning): fatal assertions drop while the effective and strong counts hold (`ASSERT_*` -> `EXPECT_*`, testify `require.*` -> `assert.*`).
  - `Assertion Bound Loosened`: the same assertion with its numeric bound moved the way that accepts more, while the count holds (`assert elapsed < 1.5` -> `< 5.0`, `pytest.approx(x, rel=1e-6)` -> `rel=1e-2`, `places=7` -> `places=2`). Read for Python (`assert` comparisons, tolerance keywords, `assertLess` / `assertGreater` / `assertAlmostEqual`), JS/TS (`toBeLessThan` / `toBeGreaterThan` and their `OrEqual` forms, `toBeCloseTo` digits, chai `below` / `above` / `most` / `least`, comparisons inside `expect(...)` / `assert(...)`), Rust (a literal that is a whole operand of a comparison in an `assert` macro, `epsilon =` style tolerances) and Go (`if x > N { t.Fatal(...) }`, `N*time.Unit` included; testify `Less` / `Greater` / `InDelta` / `InEpsilon`). Assertions are paired by their text with the literal masked; one that appears twice in a test is ambiguous and not compared. A bound held in a variable or constant, or nested in a call (`Duration::from_millis(1500)`), is not read. The finding names the line and the two values, never the assertion's text. Lifted by the same `allow-assertion-drop:` directive.
  - `Expected Value Changed In Existing Test`: the same assertion now expects a different value, while the count and strength hold (`assert_eq!(add(2, 2), 4)` -> `5`; the classic way a failing test is made to agree with changed code). An expected value is a literal that is a whole operand of an equality assertion: Rust `assert_eq!` / `assert_ne!` family (the first two arguments, not the message), a literal next to `==` / `!=` in another `assert` macro, insta inline snapshots (`@"..."`); Python `assert a == <literal>` (`!=`, `is`, `is not`), `assertEqual` / `assertNotEqual` / `assertIs` family, `snapshot("...")`; JS/TS `toBe` / `toEqual` / `toStrictEqual` / `toHaveLength` / `toThrow` / chai `equal` / `eql`, `assert.equal` / `strictEqual` / `deepEqual` family, `toMatchInlineSnapshot` strings, `===` / `==` inside `expect(...)` / `assert(...)`; Go testify `Equal` / `NotEqual` / `EqualValues` / `Exactly` / `EqualError` / `Len`, and `if got != N { t.Error(...) }`. Assertions are paired by their text with the literal masked; one that appears twice in a test is ambiguous and not compared. The finding names the line, never the assertion or its values (a string expectation is the change's own text). Lifted by the same `allow-assertion-drop:` directive, as rewriting a golden file needs `allow-golden-update:`.
  - `Self-Comparison Assertion Introduced In Existing Test` (warning, `self-comparison-assertion-introduced`): a change makes an equality assertion of an existing test compare an expression with itself (`assert_eq!(total, expected)` -> `assert_eq!(total, total)`), or adds such an assertion to it. Reported at the assertion's line, once for each one the base side did not already hold; a self-comparison that was there and is untouched is not reported, wherever its line moved. One finding for one act: when the rewrite also lowers the effective or the equality count, the test's finding is `Assertion Count Decreased In Existing Test`, at the gate's severity as before, whose message names the lines of the self-comparisons, and this warning is not added to it. The warning is reported while the counts hold: a self-comparison added beside the assertions that were there, or swapped in while another assertion is added. Forms, limits and negative controls: [Self-comparison assertions](#self-comparison-assertions) under `vacuous-tests`.
  - `Expected Exception Or Panic Widened`: the expected failure of an assertion or attribute in an existing test is widened, or no longer checked, while the count holds. Lifted by the same `allow-assertion-drop:` directive.
    - **Forms read.** Rust `#[should_panic(expected = "..")]` and `#[should_panic = ".."]`, on a `#[test]` and on a case inside `proptest!` (a case inside `quickcheck!` carries no expectation). Python `pytest.raises(..)` as a `with` item (bound with `as` or not) and in its call form `pytest.raises(E, f, args)`, with `match=` and `expected_exception=`; `raises(..)` written bare when the file imports it from pytest (`from pytest import raises`, also `raises as <name>`), and not when it comes from anywhere else; `assertRaises` / `assertRaisesRegex`; `assertWarns` / `assertWarnsRegex` and `pytest.warns(Class, match=..)` (also `expected_warning=`, the call form `pytest.warns(Class, f, ..)`, and `warns` under the name the file imports it from pytest by), which stand for each other and never for an expected exception; a tuple of classes. Java `assertThrows`, `assertThrowsExactly`, the `expected` element of `@Test(..)` whatever other elements stand beside it, and AssertJ `assertThatThrownBy(..)` / `assertThatCode(..)` with `.isInstanceOf(X.class)`, `.isExactlyInstanceOf(X.class)`, `.hasMessage(..)` (the whole message) and `.hasMessageContaining(..)`, `assertThatExceptionOfType(X.class).isThrownBy(..)` with `.withMessage(..)` / `.withMessageContaining(..)`, and `assertThatCode(..).doesNotThrowAnyException()`; AssertJ's typed entry points (`assertThatIllegalArgumentException()`, `assertThatNullPointerException()`, `assertThatIllegalStateException()`, `assertThatIOException()`, `assertThatIndexOutOfBoundsException()`, `assertThatReflectiveOperationException()`, `assertThatRuntimeException()`, `assertThatException()`), each read as `assertThatExceptionOfType` of the class it names; and `Throwable thrown = catchThrowable(code)` / `X e = catchThrowableOfType(code, X.class)` with the `assertThat(thrown)` chains the same method makes on the bound value (the call alone returns `null` when nothing is thrown, and is no expectation without one). Jest / Vitest `toThrow(..)` / `toThrowError(..)` with a class (`TypeError`, `errors.NotFound`, `new RangeError("..")`), a string, a template, a regular expression, or any other expression (a constant such as `ERR_MSG`), and their `.not` forms; Chai `expect(..).to.throw(Class, message)` and its `.not` forms, on a chain that starts at `expect(..)`, and the `should` style (`fn.should.throw(Class, message)`, `fn.should.not.throw()`), on a chain that passes through `.should`; `assert.throws(..)` / `assert.rejects(..)` / `assert.doesNotThrow(..)` / `assert.doesNotReject(..)` of Node's `assert` (the argument after the code is a class, a pattern, an object whose `name` is the class and whose `message` is the whole message, or a validating function; a string there is the assertion's own message and is not a constraint) and of Chai's `assert` (a class, then a message matcher). Whose `assert` it is comes from the file's `import` or `require` (`assert`, `node:assert`, their `/strict` forms, `chai`); an `assert` the file binds to nothing is read as Node's, and one bound to another module is not read. C# `Assert.Throws<T>` and its async, exact, MSTest and `typeof(T)` spellings, `Assert.ThrowsAny<T>` and NUnit `Assert.Catch<T>`, on a bare or a qualified assertion class (`Xunit.Assert`); NUnit `Assert.That(code, Throws.TypeOf<T>())` (the exact class), `Throws.InstanceOf<T>()` (the class or a subclass), `Throws.Exception`, `Throws.Exception.TypeOf<T>()`, the named constraints (`Throws.ArgumentException`, `Throws.ArgumentNullException`, `Throws.InvalidOperationException`, `Throws.TargetInvocationException`), `.With.Message.EqualTo(..)` (the whole message) / `.Contains(..)`, and `Throws.Nothing`; FluentAssertions `.Should().Throw<T>()` / `.ThrowExactly<T>()` with `.WithMessage(..)`, also after an awaited call (`(await act.Should().ThrowAsync<T>()).WithMessage(..)`). PHPUnit `expectException(Foo::class)`, `expectExceptionMessage(..)`, `expectExceptionMessageMatches(..)`, `expectExceptionCode(..)`, and `expectExceptionObject(new Foo("message", code))`, read as the class, message and code calls it stands for. Kotlin JUnit `assertThrows<T> { }` / `assertThrowsExactly<T> { }`, kotlin.test `assertFailsWith<T> { }` / `assertFailsWith(T::class) { }` / `assertFails { }`, Kotest `shouldThrow<T> { }` / `shouldThrowExactly<T> { }` / `shouldThrowAny { }`, and `assertDoesNotThrow { }` / `shouldNotThrowAny { }` / `shouldNotThrow<T> { }`; and the Kotest assertion on the message of the exception one of these returns, bound to a name or chained: `e.message shouldBe ".."` and `e shouldHaveMessage ".."` (the whole message), `e.message shouldContain ".."` (a part), another `should..` matcher on `e.message` (kept as written), each also as a call (`e.message.shouldBe("..")`). Ruby RSpec `expect { }.to raise_error(Class, message)` (also `raise_exception`, `.with_message(..)`, and `.not_to` / `.to_not`; a string is the whole message), and Minitest / test-unit `assert_raises(Class, ..) { }`. C++ googletest `EXPECT_THROW(statement, Type)` / `ASSERT_THROW`, `EXPECT_ANY_THROW` / `ASSERT_ANY_THROW`, and `EXPECT_NO_THROW` / `ASSERT_NO_THROW`; Catch2 and doctest `REQUIRE_THROWS(expr)`, `REQUIRE_THROWS_AS(expr, Type)`, `REQUIRE_THROWS_WITH(expr, message)` (a string is the whole message; any other matcher is kept as written), Catch2 `REQUIRE_THROWS_MATCHES(expr, Type, matcher)`, doctest `REQUIRE_THROWS_WITH_AS(expr, message, Type)`, and `REQUIRE_NOTHROW(expr)`, the same under `CHECK_`, and with doctest's `_MESSAGE` suffix, whose last argument is the assertion's own message. Go: `errors.Is(err, target)` / `errors.As(err, &target)` negated in the condition of an `if` whose block fails the test (`if !errors.Is(err, ErrGone) { t.Fatal(..) }`, also as a side of `||`), and inside testify's `True(..)`; testify `ErrorIs` / `ErrorAs` (the sentinel, or the type the target is declared with in the function), `ErrorContains` (a part of the message), `EqualError` (the whole message), `Error`, and `NoError` (no error); `Panics`, `PanicsWithValue` / `PanicsWithError` (the value or the whole message), and `NotPanics`; each also with a trailing `f`, on the `assert` or `require` package, on a suite's receiver and its `Require()` / `Assert()`, and on a local bound to an assertion object (`r := require.New(t)`); gocheck `c.Assert(err, ErrorMatches, pattern)`, `c.Assert(f, Panics, value)` and `c.Assert(f, PanicMatches, pattern)` (also `c.Check`), where a pattern must match the whole message. Whose `assert`, `require`, `errors` or checker it is comes from the file's imports: testify's packages and `errors` (also `github.com/pkg/errors`, `golang.org/x/xerrors`) under the name the file imports them by, a gocheck checker written bare under a dot import of the package or under the name it is imported by; the same name bound to another package is not read. Every expectation on one error value is one site (`require.ErrorIs(t, err, ErrGone)` -> `require.Error(t, err)` is the same site edited), and a panic never stands for a returned error. Swift XCTest `XCTAssertThrowsError(expr) { error in .. }`, whose type is the first `error as? T` / `error is T` the closure applies to its parameter (`$0` when it names none; `error as NSError` holds for every error and names no type), and `XCTAssertNoThrow(expr)`, in a file that imports `XCTest`; Swift Testing `#expect(throws: T.self) { }` / `#require(throws:)`, with a type or an error value (`SutError.negative`, read as its type and the value), and `throws: Never.self`, which states that nothing is thrown, in a file that imports `Testing`. Scala: ScalaTest and munit `intercept[T] { }` / `assertThrows[T] { }`, ScalaTest matchers `the [T] thrownBy { } should have message ".."` (the whole message), `a [T] should be thrownBy { }` / `an [T] ..`, and `noException should be thrownBy { }`; specs2 `code must throwA[T]` / `throwAn[T]`, with a message argument (a string, or `message = ".."`: a pattern searched for in the message) or an exception value (`throwA(new T(..))`, read as its class). They are read by name where the callee is written bare, since a ScalaTest or specs2 assertion is inherited from a trait and no import names it; `obj.intercept[T] { }` is not read. Objective-C XCTest `XCTAssertThrows(expr)`, `XCTAssertThrowsSpecific(expr, Class)`, `XCTAssertThrowsSpecificNamed(expr, Class, name)` (the exception's name, compared whole), and `XCTAssertNoThrow(expr)` / `XCTAssertNoThrowSpecific(expr, Class)` / `XCTAssertNoThrowSpecificNamed(expr, Class, name)`; they are macros a header brings in, and are read by name.
    - **Whose `Assert.Throws<T>`.** `Assert.Throws<T>` is exact in xUnit and NUnit (NUnit's `Assert.Catch<T>` and xUnit's `Assert.ThrowsAny<T>` accept subclasses). In MSTest it exists from 3.8 only and accepts subclasses, while `Assert.ThrowsException<T>` and `Assert.ThrowsExactly<T>` are exact in every version. Whose `Assert` a file's is comes from its `using` directives, read from the syntax tree on each side of the change (`global using` included; an alias and a `using static` bind another name and are not read). **(a)** `using Xunit;` or `using NUnit.Framework;`: `Assert.Throws<T>` is exact, so `Assert.ThrowsException<T>` -> `Assert.Throws<T>` in a file that is, or has become, an xUnit or NUnit file keeps the exact class and is not reported: it is a move to another framework. **(b)** `using Microsoft.VisualStudio.TestTools.UnitTesting;`: `Assert.Throws<T>` accepts subclasses, with `Assert.ThrowsExactly` elsewhere in the file or without, so `Assert.ThrowsException<T>` -> `Assert.Throws<T>` and `Assert.ThrowsExactly<T>` -> `Assert.Throws<T>` are reported as a loss of exactness, and `Assert.Throws<ArgumentNullException>` -> `Assert.Throws<ArgumentException>` is a widening. **(c)** No directive names one of them (global usings in another file, a fragment), or directives name both kinds: the calls decide. `Assert.Throws<T>` accepts subclasses in a file that calls `Assert.ThrowsExactly` (or `ThrowsExactlyAsync`), a name of MSTest 3.8 and later, on either side of the change, and when the expectation it replaces is an `Assert.ThrowsExactly<T>` or an `Assert.ThrowsException<T>`, names only MSTest has; otherwise it is exact. *Known limit*: under (c), a file that never calls `Assert.ThrowsExactly` on either side reads an `Assert.Throws<T>` that stands on both sides as exact, so a move to a parent class there is not reported.
    - **Forms not read.** AssertJ `catchThrowable` whose value is asserted on through anything but `assertThat(value)` (`assertThat(thrown.getMessage())`, `assertEquals(.., thrown.getMessage())`); a kotlin.test or JUnit assertion on the returned exception (`assertEquals("..", e.message)`); Catch2 and doctest `WARN_` macros and `REQUIRE_THROWS_MATCHES` matchers beyond their text. Go's `err == nil` / `err != nil` comparisons, testify `NotErrorIs` / `NotErrorAs` and gocheck `IsNil` / `NotNil` on an error are counted as assertions and not read as expected failures. Swift: the case a closure compares the error with (`XCTAssertEqual(error as? E, .negative)` gives the type `E` only), a `switch` or `if case` on the error, and the value `#expect(throws:)` returns. Scala: munit `interceptMessage[T]`, an assertion on the value `intercept` or `the [T] thrownBy` returns (`e.getMessage should include (..)`), and specs2 `must not(throwA[T])` and `.like { .. }`. A form that is not read is counted as an assertion, and what it expects is not compared.
    - **What is a widening.** Dropping a pattern or message matcher (`#[should_panic(expected = "...")]` -> `#[should_panic]`, `pytest.raises(..., match=...)` -> without `match=`, `toThrow("msg")` -> `toThrow()`); replacing it by one that accepts every message (an empty string, a pattern that is only `.*`, a `WithMessage("*")`, a PHP pattern whose body is only `.*`); shortening a plain string to a part of itself (`"negative value"` -> `"value"`); requiring a part of the message where the whole message was required (`hasMessage("x")` -> `hasMessageContaining("x")`). Dropping the exception type (`toThrow(TypeError)` -> `toThrow()`); with a message matcher put in its place (`toThrow(TypeError)` -> `toThrow("msg")`) the finding says so, because any class with that message now passes. Moving the type to a root or general name (`Exception`, `BaseException`, `Throwable`, `Error`, `StandardError`, with or without a namespace; in Ruby `Exception` and `StandardError` only, in C++ `std::exception` only, in Swift `Error` / `any Error` only, in Objective-C `NSException` only; Go has no such name), to a parent class in the language's standard hierarchy, or to a base class the test's own file declares (below). Adding a class to the accepted ones (`ValueError` -> `(ValueError, TypeError)`). Replacing an exact-type assertion by one that accepts subclasses (C# `Assert.Throws<T>` -> `Assert.ThrowsAny<T>` or `Assert.Catch<T>`, `ThrowExactly<T>` -> `Throw<T>`, `Throws.TypeOf<T>()` -> `Throws.InstanceOf<T>()`; Java `assertThrowsExactly` -> `assertThrows`, `isExactlyInstanceOf` -> `isInstanceOf`; Kotlin `shouldThrowExactly<T>` -> `shouldThrow<T>`). For a negated expectation that passes on any failure of another kind (`.not.toThrow(..)`, Chai's `.not.throw(..)` and `assert.doesNotThrow(..)`, RSpec `.not_to raise_error(..)`) the direction is reversed: naming a type or a message where there was none is the widening, and removing one is not.
    - **Standard hierarchies.** A fixed table per language (`src/ast/exception_tables.rs`), each pinned by a unit test to a fixture under `tests/fixtures/exception_hierarchy/` whose header names the runtime and the command that produced it: Python's built-in exceptions; Java's public `Throwable` classes of `java.lang`, `java.lang.reflect`, `java.io`, `java.net`, `java.nio`, `java.nio.charset`, `java.nio.file`, `java.util`, `java.util.concurrent`, `java.util.regex`, `java.util.zip`, `java.time` and `java.time.format` (Kotlin's and Scala's forms use the same table); JavaScript's native errors and `DOMException`; PHP's predefined, SPL and JSON exceptions; Ruby's top-level core exceptions; the C++ standard library's exceptions; and .NET's `System`, `System.IO`, `System.Collections.Generic` and `System.Threading.Tasks` exceptions, whose fixture was entered from the API reference and not produced by a runtime. A class is read as the standard one when it is written bare, or under a qualifier of the standard library: `builtins.` in Python; one of the packages above, or `kotlin.` or `scala.`, in Java, Kotlin and Scala; `System.`, `System.IO.`, `System.Collections.Generic.`, `System.Threading.` and `System.Threading.Tasks.` in C# (with or without `global::`); `globalThis.`, `window.`, `global.` and `self.` in JavaScript; the root namespace in PHP (`\LogicException`) and Ruby (`::KeyError`); `std::` and `std::filesystem::` in C++. Python's `IOError` / `EnvironmentError` / `WindowsError` are `OSError`. **A class under any other qualifier is a class of the project** (`errors.TimeoutError`, `my.pkg.IOException`, `App\InvalidArgumentException`, `mylib::out_of_range`): the table says nothing about it, whatever its last segment is, and it is still the same class as its unqualified spelling. An exact-type assertion (`Assert.Throws<T>`, `ThrowExactly<T>`, `Throws.TypeOf<T>()`, `assertThrowsExactly`, `isExactlyInstanceOf`, `shouldThrowExactly<T>`) accepts no subclass, so for it only a move to a root or general name is reported.
    - **Classes the file declares.** The class declarations of the test's own file are read from the syntax tree, each with the parents it names (`class OrderError(AppError)`, `class A extends B`, `class A : B`, `class A < B`, `class A : public B`), in Python, Java, C#, JavaScript / TypeScript, PHP, Kotlin, Scala, Ruby and C++. An expectation moved from a class to a class above it in that relation is a widening (`pytest.raises(OrderError)` -> `pytest.raises(AppError)`), through any number of declarations, and on into the standard table when a declared class extends a standard one (`class MissingKey(KeyError)`, then `MissingKey` -> `LookupError`). A class the file declares has the parents it is declared with, also under a standard name: a file's own `class TimeoutError(Exception)` is not the built-in one. Only an unqualified name is looked up among the file's classes. Each side of the change is read with its own file, and a relation that holds on either side counts: when the change itself re-parents the class, the side that shows the head class above the base class is the one that decides.
    - **Languages without a table.** Go, Swift and Objective-C have no standard table and no declared-class reading: a sentinel, an error type or an exception class is compared by its name. What is reported there is the name removed (`require.ErrorIs(t, err, ErrGone)` -> `require.Error(t, err)`, a closure that no longer checks `error is SutError`, `XCTAssertThrowsSpecific` -> `XCTAssertThrows`), a move to the language's general name (`#expect(throws: SutError.self)` -> `(any Error).self`, `SutException` -> `NSException`), and a message or value matcher dropped or loosened (`EqualError` -> `ErrorContains`, `PanicsWithValue` -> `Panics`, `#expect(throws: SutError.negative)` -> `SutError.self`, `XCTAssertThrowsSpecificNamed` -> `XCTAssertThrowsSpecific`). One name replaced by another is a substitution and is not reported. An expectation turned into its negation (`Error` -> `NoError`, `XCTAssertThrowsError` -> `XCTAssertNoThrow`, `throws: Never.self`) is another assertion, not a wider one, in every language.
    - **Unknown relations.** A class declared in another file, or imported, is in no table and in no declaration the gate reads. One class replaced by one other whose relation to it is not known (`ValueError` -> `TypeError`, `OrderError` -> `PaymentError`, an imported `OrderError` -> its imported base class `AppError`) is a substitution and is not reported. A matcher replaced by a different one that is not a part of it, a pattern replaced by another pattern, a whole message replaced by another whole message, and a matcher that is not a string literal replaced by another expression are not reported either.
    - **Not a widening.** A narrower class, fewer classes, a reordered tuple, a qualified spelling of the same class, a longer message, an exact form replacing a subclass-accepting one, a whole message required where a part of it was. A matcher given up for a narrower class (`pytest.raises(Exception, match="neg")` -> `pytest.raises(ValueError)`) is a trade and is not reported. The trade is not excused the other way: a matcher gained beside a wider class (`pytest.raises(ValueError)` -> `pytest.raises(Exception, match="neg")`) accepts failures the base expectation rejected, so the type is reported as widened and the finding says a matcher was added beside it.
    - **Pairing.** The base and head expectations of one test are compared as two sets, in three steps. (1) An expectation written the same on both sides (skeleton with the type and matcher masked, kind, type and matcher) is unchanged, however many times it occurs and in whatever order. (2) Of the rest, a skeleton left exactly once on each side is the same assertion edited in place, and the two are compared. (3) What remains was rewritten beyond its skeleton (an edited `with` block or lambda, a renamed `as` binding, a site replaced by another): each base expectation needs a head expectation of its own that accepts no more than it did, and the assignment satisfying the most base expectations is taken. One left without is reported against a remaining head expectation, or as no longer checked when head has none left. A removed attribute (`#[should_panic]`, `@Test(expected = ..)`) is not a dropped expectation: the test must then pass without the failure. An added expectation is never a finding. Two sites that are both rewritten and trade strictness are not reported: every base expectation still has a head expectation of its own that accepts no more, so the test expects no less than it did; which call each expectation guards is not compared.
    - **A dropped expectation.** It is reported here only while the assertion count holds; with a lower count it is the `Assertion Count Decreased` finding. The finding names the class (or the message) that is no longer checked and the directive that lifts a replacement made on purpose. It also fires on a behaviour change that is legitimate: code that used to fail and now returns a value. One shape of that is not reported, in Python and JavaScript / TypeScript: the dropped site guarded exactly one call (the `with` block is that one call statement, or the callback's body is that call, awaited or not), and the head side of the same test gained an equality assertion with a literal expected value (the assertions the expected-value reader records: `assert f(-1) == 0`, `self.assertEqual(f(-1), 0)`, `expect(f(-1)).toBe(0)`) that makes the same call. Two calls are the same when their syntax nodes have the same tokens in the same order: the same callee and the same arguments, however they are spaced or broken over lines, comments left out, the content of a string literal kept as written. An assertion on other arguments or another callee, an assertion the base side already had, a guarded block that does anything besides the one call, the call form `pytest.raises(E, f, args)`, and an assertion that is not an equality with a literal are all still reported; `allow-assertion-drop: <test> <reason>` lifts them.
  - `Mocking Increased Without Stronger Assertions` (warning): an existing test gains test doubles (`Mock()`, `jest.fn`, `when(`, `.Setup(`, ...; `mock_setup_fns` extends the vocabulary) while its equality / pattern assertions and its assertions on real output do not grow. That is the shape of an integration failure mocked away. Doubles added together with a stronger assertion on the result are not reported.
  - `Assertion Failure Caught Inside Test`: an assertion kept in place but enclosed in a handler within the test function that catches its failure type and swallows it (neither re-raising nor failing the test nor asserting on the caught error; Python `try: assert ... except AssertionError:` / `except Exception:` / `except* AssertionError:` and `with contextlib.suppress(AssertionError):`, Rust `let _ = std::panic::catch_unwind(...)`, JS/TS `try { expect(...) } catch {}` and `<promise>.catch(() => {})` after a chain that asserts, Java `try { assertEquals(...) } catch (AssertionError e) {}` including `try`-with-resources, Kotlin `try { assertEquals(...) } catch (e: AssertionError) {}` and `runCatching { assertEquals(...) }` with the result unused, C# `try { Assert.Equal(...) } catch (Exception) {}`, Go `defer func() { recover() }()` before a check that panics, and in Java, Kotlin, JS/TS and Python a `finally` block that returns). The neutralized assertion cannot fail the test and drops out of effective assertions; reported on the assertion's line with the handler's line cited. On a test that exists on both sides, a swallowed assertion is new by its position inside the test, not by its line in the file: lines added above the test, or the test moved, report nothing, and an edit inside the test that moves a swallowed assertion without changing its distance to its handler reports nothing. A test the change adds is read the same way and counts as examined. A swallowed assertion is counted by its syntax node, not by its line: two assertions on one line inside one handler are two swallowed assertions, and the nested calls of one assertion (`expect(x)` inside `expect(x).toBe(1)`) are one. A swallowed assertion that is also a tautology is taken out of the effective count once, so a test with one real assertion beside it keeps one effective assertion.
    - **Which handlers catch the failure.** The type the handler names is read from the syntax tree, by its last segment (`java.io.IOException` is `IOException`), never from the text of the clause. Java and Kotlin: `AssertionError`, `Error` and `Throwable` always catch it; `Exception`, `RuntimeException` and the JDK classes under them, and the `Error` classes beside `AssertionError` (`OutOfMemoryError`, ...), never do, so `catch (Exception e) {}` and `catch (IOException e) {}` around an assertion are not reported. C#: `Exception` and a `catch` with no type always catch it; the .NET classes an assertion failure is not an instance of (`IOException`, `InvalidOperationException`, `ArgumentException`, ...) never do. A type on neither list (a class of the project, or a subclass of the failure type such as JUnit `AssertionFailedError`, NUnit `AssertionException`, xUnit `XunitException`, MSTest `AssertFailedException`) may catch it, and a handler for it that swallows is reported: a project class can extend the failure type, and passing an unknown name would leave a way to hide a failure behind a new class. A multi-catch reaches as far as its widest type. Handlers are read in order: the first that always catches the failure decides, so a broad empty handler after one that rethrows is not reported; a handler that may catch it, and a C# handler with a `when` filter, is passed over when it does not swallow. Python: `AssertionError`, `Exception`, `BaseException` and a bare `except:` always catch it (a multi-line tuple and `except*` included); the other built-in exception classes (`KeyError`, `OSError`, `ValueError`, ...; `IOError` and the other aliases of `OSError`) and a short list of standard-library ones (`json.JSONDecodeError`, `subprocess.CalledProcessError`, `urllib.error.URLError`, ...) never do, because `AssertionError` is not among their subclasses. A class the file declares is read through its bases: it never catches the failure when every base is such a standard class, `Exception` or `BaseException`, or another class of the file that never does; it may when a base is `AssertionError`, a class that may, or a class the file does not declare. Any other name (a class of the project or of a library) may catch it, as in Java, Kotlin and C#. A Python handler that may catch it is reported only when its body does nothing with the failure: every statement is `pass`, `...`, `continue`, or a logging call (`print(..)`, `warnings.warn(..)`, a `debug` / `info` / `warning` / `warn` / `error` / `exception` / `critical` / `fatal` / `log` call on `log`, `logging`, or a name ending in `logger` or `_log`, in any case: `logger`, `self.log`, `LOG`, `app_logger`); a handler that re-raises, fails, asserts or does anything else (assigns, returns, calls another function) is not. `contextlib.suppress(...)` is read by the same classes. JS/TS: any `catch` catches it.
    - **What is not swallowing.** Expected-exception idioms (`pytest.raises`, `assertThrows`, `Assert.Throws`); handlers that re-raise or throw; calls that fail the test (`pytest.fail`, `self.fail`, `fail()`, `Assert.Fail`, in JS/TS also `done(err)` with an argument and `reject(...)`); assertions on the caught error inside the handler; a `finally` with no `catch` that does not return. NUnit `Assert.Pass()`, `Assert.Ignore()` and `Assert.Inconclusive()` end the test without failing it, so a handler that calls one of them still swallows. Python soft assertions: a handler that keeps the caught error (`errors.append(e)`, `last = e`) when a statement after the `try` that names what it was kept in asserts, raises or fails. Python retry loops: a `try` in a loop that leaves it on success (`break` or `return`) when the loop's `else` raises or fails, or, when success returns, a `raise`, a failing call or `assert False` follows the loop. C# assertions are `Assert.<Method>(...)` invocations (and those of a class named `...Assert`: `ClassicAssert`, `StringAssert`, `CollectionAssert`, a project's own) read from the callee, so a string or a comment that mentions `Assert.` is neither an assertion nor a failing call.
    - **Rust `catch_unwind`.** The result is followed through the syntax tree to where it ends up; the closure may be wrapped in `AssertUnwindSafe(..)`. Something looks at the outcome when `unwrap`, `expect`, `unwrap_err` or `expect_err` is called on it, when `?` or `return` hands it on or it is the value of the test function, when an asserting or panicking macro names it (`assert!(r.is_err())`, `assert!(matches!(r, Err(_)))`), and when an `if` / `if let` / `match` on it panics, asserts, returns or calls `resume_unwind` in the branch taken for a failure: the consequence of `if r.is_err()`, `if !r.is_ok()` and `if let Err(..) = r`, the `else` of `if r.is_ok()` and `if let Ok(..) = r`, the `Err(..)` arm of a `match` (or, when no arm names `Err`, an arm that does not name `Ok`). It is thrown away, and the assertions in the closure reported, as a statement of its own, in `let _ =` or `_ =`, in `drop(..)`, in a binding nothing looks at (a comment naming the binding, or a check on another binding with a longer name, is not a look), in an `if` / `match` no branch of which fails, and in one that fails only in the branch taken when the closure did not panic (`if r.is_ok() { panic!(..) }`, `Ok(()) => panic!(..)`): there the test fails when the assertion held and passes when it did not. An `if` with no `else` is not read as failing in the code that follows it; a condition of another shape, a chain of `let` conditions and a `while` are judged by whether any branch fails. `assert!(r.is_err())` and `r.unwrap_err()` are read as looking at the outcome, as before.
    - **Kotlin `runCatching`.** Reported when the call is a statement of its own, when only calls that keep the failure are chained on it (`getOrNull()`, `onFailure { println(it) }`), or when it is bound to a name never used again. Not reported with `getOrThrow()`, with a chained callback that throws, fails or asserts, with the bound name used later, or when the result is passed on.
    - **Go `recover()`.** `t.Fatal`, `t.Error` and the testify `require.*` / `assert.*` functions do not panic: `t.FailNow` ends the test through `runtime.Goexit`, which a `recover()` does not stop. An assertion after a recovering `defer` is therefore not caught, and a test whose assertions follow one is not vacuous. What a deferred `recover()` catches is a check that panics: a `panic(..)` written in the function after the `defer` statement, and a call there to a function declared in the same file whose body calls `panic(..)`. It is reported when the deferred function (a function literal, or a function the file declares, deferred by name) calls `recover()` and does not fail the test: no `panic(..)`, no `Fatal` / `Fatalf` / `FailNow` / `Fail` / `Failf` / `Errorf` / `Error(..)` call, no `require.*` / `assert.*` call (`require.Nil(t, r)` fails the test when the panic happened, and is not itself a caught assertion), no `s.Require().X(..)` / `s.Assert().X(..)`, no configured helper. `err.Error()` (no argument) and `fmt.Errorf(..)` do not fail the test. The `defer` belongs to the function it is written in: a function literal (a `t.Run` subtest) is read with its own deferred calls. A `panic(..)` written in the test is reported but was never counted as an assertion, so it does not lower the test's count.
    - **A `finally` that returns.** In Java, Kotlin, JS/TS and Python a `return` that is a statement of the `finally` block itself replaces whatever the `try` raised, whatever the handlers do: the assertions of the `try` body are reported, on the `finally` line. A `return` under a condition, or in a lambda or nested function of the block, is not read. C# does not compile a `return` inside `finally`.
    - **Callbacks inside the `try`.** An assertion in a function passed as an argument inside the `try` body is read when the function it is passed to is known to run it before returning: JS/TS `forEach`, `map`, `filter`, `some`, `every`, `reduce`, `find` (called as a method); Java `forEach`; Kotlin `let`, `run`, `apply`, `also`, `with`, `forEach`, `use`; C# `ForEach`. An assertion in any other callback (`setTimeout`, an event handler, `executor.submit`, `Task.Run`, `thread { }`) is not read: whether it runs before the `try` ends is not known. The match is by method name, so a project method of one of these names is read the same way.
    - **Named promise handlers.** JS/TS `<promise>.catch(handler)` is judged by the body of `handler` when the file declares exactly one function of that name (`function handler(e) { }`, or a `const` / `let` / `var` bound to an arrow or function expression). A name the file does not declare (`.catch(done)`), declares twice, or a member (`.catch(h.ignore)`) is not read.
    - **Configured helpers.** A call to a helper listed in `assert_helper_fns` (of `assertion-reduction` or `vacuous-tests`; matched by the whole callee or its last `::` / `.` segment, as the counters match it) is an assertion here too: inside a swallowing handler it is reported, and in a handler body it counts as asserting on the caught error. A Rust macro listed in `extra_assert_macros` is read the same way inside a `catch_unwind` closure. In Go a configured helper is not known to panic, so one after a recovering `defer` is not reported.
    - *Known limits*: an assertion inside a callback handed to a function that is not on the list above is not read; a `finally` that returns under a condition is not read; a Go check that panics in a function declared in another file, or called as a method, is not read; Ruby, PHP, Swift, Scala, C/C++ and Objective-C handlers are not read by this finding.
  - `Test Cases Reduced In Parametrized Test`: removing cases from a parametrized or table-driven test reduces verification coverage even when the test function itself survives and keeps its assertions. Statically extracts case counts across Python (`@pytest.mark.parametrize` list, tuple, and stacked Cartesian products, on the test, on its class, or in a `pytestmark` of its class or module; `parametrize` is read through what the file binds it to, which is `pytest.mark.parametrize` under the name the file imports `pytest` by (`import pytest as pt`), `mark.parametrize` after `from pytest import mark`, and a module-level name assigned either (`parametrize = pytest.mark.parametrize`), while a function merely named `parametrize` (`harness.parametrize(..)`, a `mark` imported from another module, a `pytest` the file defines) is not a case source), JS/TS (`test.each` array and tagged template literals, multiplied by the `describe.each` tables around the test, where `it`, `test` or `describe` is the runner's: its global, a name the file imports from a runner (`vitest`, `@jest/globals`, `bun:test`, `node:test`, `mocha`, `@playwright/test`, `jest-circus`, `@japa/runner`), or a name derived from one (`const test = base.extend({..})`); under a name the file declares itself, and on any other receiver, `each` is a project function and its argument is not a case list; under a name the file imports from any other module (`import { test } from './fixtures'`), which may hand on the runner's own function, the test has a case source that is not counted, so the note for a non-literal case source is emitted and a counted list that moves under such a name is reported as no longer a literal list; a template is its header row and one case per line of its text after it, whatever the number of columns, a line break inside a `${..}` value is part of that value, and a template holding only its header row has no cases), Go (every `[]struct`, array or map table in the test body added up; a slice, array or map of a type whose name holds the whole word `case`, `cases`, `testcase` or `testcases`, and for a slice or array `test` or `tests` (`testCase`, `addTests`, `pkg.TestCase`, a pointer to one), wherever it stands; a slice, array or map of any other struct type declared in the same file only when the test ranges over it, since such a value is as often an expected result or a fixture as a table; and a package-level `var` holding such a table when the test body names it, so a table moved out of the test function keeps its rows, in the test's file or, when that file is part of the change, in another file of its package; a `map[string]struct{}` set is not a table), Java and Kotlin (`@ValueSource`, `@CsvSource` with its `value` and `textBlock` forms, `@NullSource`, `@EmptySource`, `@NullAndEmptySource`, and in Java `@EnumSource(names = ..)`; several sources on one test add up; a Java `@ValueSource` that names a constant, `strings = NAME` or `ints = {A, B}`, is one case per name, since an annotation element takes constant expressions and no array is one), C# (`[InlineData]`, `[TestCase]`, `[DataRow]`), and Rust (`rstest` `#[case]`, `#[values]` combinations counted by argument and multiplied by the cases beside them, and `#[test_case(..)]` of the `test-case` crate; the attributes are told by their path, `case`, `case::name`, `values` and the same under `rstest::`, so `#[case_x(..)]` is another attribute). A Go type is named for cases by a whole word of its name: `Showcase` and `Contestant` are not rows of cases. A case that the change turns into a comment is a dropped case in every one of these: a commented-out row, value or attribute, and a `#` line in a `@CsvSource` text block. So is a C# row whose attribute gains `Skip`, `Ignore`, `IgnoreReason` or `IgnoreMessage`. A case that leaves a test is read as moved, and recorded in gate notes without failing, only when the same case arrives in another test of the same file: on that test's head side and not on its base side. Cases are compared by content, the text of the row or element taken from the syntax tree with whitespace, comments and a trailing comma left out, and one arrival stands for one dropped case. Only as many cases as the count went down by must be found, so a row edited in place beside moved rows is not a dropped one. The file holding as many cases as before excuses nothing: three cases dropped beside an unrelated new test of three are reported. The note gives how many dropped cases were found moved and how many were not (`2 of 3 dropped case(s) found moved to another test of the file, 1 not found`), beside the finding when some were not. A case list that is not literal cannot be compared, so such a test neither supplies nor receives this: a test whose list became non-literal is reported even when its rows appear elsewhere. The combinations of multiplied sources (stacked `parametrize`, `describe.each` around `test.each`, `#[case]` beside `#[values]`) are compared as combinations up to 4096 of them; past that the count is compared and nothing is read as moved. Non-literal case sources (dynamic iterators, generator functions, `@MethodSource`, `[MemberData]`, `[DynamicData]`, an `@EnumSource` that does not list its constants, a Java `@CsvSource` or `@EnumSource` whose value is a named constant, and a list holding a spread or splat element: `[row, ...more]` in JS/TS, `[1, *more]` in Python, `intArrayOf(1, *more)` in Kotlin) emit an explanatory note that static case counting is unavailable; when the base side had two or more literal cases and the head side of the same test has no literal count, because its source became non-literal or because the test is no longer parametrized, the finding is reported with the base count. A literal list that gains a spread or splat while keeping every row is such a change: the list can no longer be counted, and it is reported as no longer a literal list. A list that is non-literal on both sides is not judged, and neither is a spread removed from a list that had one. Lifted by `allow-case-drop: <test-name> <reason>` (lifts only `test-cases-reduced`) or `allow-assertion-drop: <test-name> <reason>` (lifts every assertion-reduction finding including it).
  - `Test Helper Function Weakened`: a shared assertion helper function located in a test-support path (such as `tests/helpers.py`, `conftest.py`, `tests/common/mod.rs`, Go `_test.go` or `testutil`, `test-utils.ts`) loses assertions or failure exits (`raise`, `panic!`, `throw`, etc.), or is deleted, even when test files that invoke it are untouched in the diff. A test-support path is a test path other than Cargo's `examples/` and `benches/`: no test calls into those, so a function there is not an assertion helper.
